========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, Dr. Lee. Um… I guess the biggest reason I’m here is that at work, on the floor, I’m losing my train of thought constantly, and it’s starting to feel like I can’t trust my own brain in the middle of the day.

Like, I’ll be trying to work through a patient flow issue — figuring out who needs to go where, or what needs to happen next — and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone near me asks another nurse a question, or a call light goes off, and it’s like whatever I was holding in my head just disappears. Not just “oh, give me a second,” but genuinely gone, and I have to retrace everything to figure out what I was doing. It happens over and over, and because the floor is basically constant interruptions, I feel like I’m always getting knocked off track.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, it’s definitely not just recent. I mean, work is where it feels most scary now because there are actual patients involved, but when I think back, it’s been there for as long as I can remember.

Even in elementary school, teachers would write things like, “She’s bright, but careless,” or that I wasn’t turning things in. And I remember constantly losing worksheets — like I’d do them, sometimes, or at least start them, and then they’d just vanish into my desk or backpack. Homework was a whole thing too. My mom basically had to sit with me every night and keep redirecting me, like, “Okay, what’s next? Did you put it in your folder? Did you put the folder in your backpack?” Otherwise I’d either drift off or forget to turn it in even if I’d done it.

So… yeah, the setting is different now, but that pattern of losing the thread or needing someone to keep me on track has kind of always been there.

It happens over and over, and because the floor is basically constant interruptions, I feel like I’m always getting knocked off track.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed this kind of difficulty holding onto your train of thought throughout your life, even back in school or earlier jobs, or is this something that's really only started recently?\n\nPatient: No, it’s definitely not just recent. I mean, work is where it feels most scary now because there are actual patients involved, but when I think back, it’s been there for as long as I can remember.\n\nEven in elementary school, teachers would write things like, “She’s bright, but careless,” or that I wasn’t turning things in. And I remember constantly losing worksheets — like I’d do them, sometimes, or at least start them, and then they’d just vanish into my desk or backpack. Homework was a whole thing too. My mom basically had to sit with me every night and keep redirecting me, like, “Okay, what’s next? Did you put it in your folder? Did you put the folder in your backpack?” Otherwise I’d either drift off or forget to turn it in even if I’d done it.\n\nSo… yeah, the setting is different now, but that pattern of losing the thread or needing someone to keep me on track has kind of always been there.\n\nDoctor: Aside from these attention and focus issues, have you been experiencing persistent low mood, loss of interest in things you used to enjoy, or excessive worry over the past couple of weeks?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? At least not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think. I mean, I get frustrated and overwhelmed because I feel like I’m constantly messing something up or having to work twice as hard to keep up, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been persistently sad for weeks or that I’ve stopped caring about things I normally like.\n\nAnd worry… I mean, I do worry about work and whether I’m missing something, but I’m not sure if that’s “excessive worry” or just reacting to what’s happening, if that makes sense. It doesn’t feel like the main thing is mood or anxiety. It feels more like I’m exhausted from trying to keep my head organized all the time.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often feel physically restless, fidgety, or have trouble sitting still, or find yourself acting or speaking impulsively without thinking it through?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`.   },

Um… I don’t really know that I’d describe myself that way, no. Like, I’m not usually the person who can’t stay in a chair or is pacing around because I physically can’t stop moving. I mean, I’ll shift around or play with a pen sometimes, but I always thought that was just normal, especially during long meetings or charting or whatever.

And impulsive speaking… I’m trying to think. I don’t think people usually experience me as blurting things out or interrupting a ton. If anything, I’m more likely to be kind of stuck in my head trying to organize what I’m going to say before I say it. Sometimes I’ll talk too much once I get going — like I’m probably doing right now — but it doesn’t feel like I’m saying reckless things without thinking, exactly.

So… no, I don’t think the restless or impulsive part is the main thing for me. Or at least it’s not something I’ve noticed the way I notice the focus stuff.

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you noticed this kind of difficulty holding onto your train of thought throughout your life, even back in school or earlier jobs, or is this something that's really only started recently?\n\nPatient: No, it’s definitely not just recent. I mean, work is where it feels most scary now because there are actual patients involved, but when I think back, it’s been there for as long as I can remember.\n\nEven in elementary school, teachers would write things like, “She’s bright, but careless,” or that I wasn’t turning things in. And I remember constantly losing worksheets — like I’d do them, sometimes, or at least start them, and then they’d just vanish into my desk or backpack. Homework was a whole thing too. My mom basically had to sit with me every night and keep redirecting me, like, “Okay, what’s next? Did you put it in your folder? Did you put the folder in your backpack?” Otherwise I’d either drift off or forget to turn it in even if I’d done it.\n\nSo… yeah, the setting is different now, but that pattern of losing the thread or needing someone to keep me on track has kind of always been there.\n\nDoctor: Aside from these attention and focus issues, have you been experiencing persistent low mood, loss of interest in things you used to enjoy, or excessive worry over the past couple of weeks?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? At least not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think. I mean, I get frustrated and overwhelmed because I feel like I’m constantly messing something up or having to work twice as hard to keep up, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been persistently sad for weeks or that I’ve stopped caring about things I normally like.\n\nAnd worry… I mean, I do worry about work and whether I’m missing something, but I’m not sure if that’s “excessive worry” or just reacting to what’s happening, if that makes sense. It doesn’t feel like the main thing is mood or anxiety. It feels more like I’m exhausted from trying to keep my head organized all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically restless, fidgety, or have trouble sitting still, or find yourself acting or speaking impulsively without thinking it through?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know that I’d describe myself that way, no. Like, I’m not usually the person who can’t stay in a chair or is pacing around because I physically can’t stop moving. I mean, I’ll shift around or play with a pen sometimes, but I always thought that was just normal, especially during long meetings or charting or whatever.\n\nAnd impulsive speaking… I’m trying to think. I don’t think people usually experience me as blurting things out or interrupting a ton. If anything, I’m more likely to be kind of stuck in my head trying to organize what I’m going to say before I say it. Sometimes I’ll talk too much once I get going — like I’m probably doing right now — but it doesn’t feel like I’m saying reckless things without thinking, exactly.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think the restless or impulsive part is the main thing for me. Or at least it’s not something I’ve noticed the way I notice the focus stuff.\n\n[Last Question]\nOutside of work, do you notice these same kinds of attention or focus problems causing issues in other areas of your life right now, like at home, in relationships, or when driving?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / registered_nurse / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Daily_Activities_Forgetfulness\nmanifestation: \"I forget things that should be routine, like calling a family member back, following up on a missing order, paying a bill at home, or showing up for an appointment, even when I meant to do it and sometimes even after I set a reminder.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, definitely. It’s not just work. Work is just where the consequences feel bigger, so it’s more obvious and more embarrassing.

At home it’s things that should be so routine that I feel ridiculous even saying them. Like I’ll fully intend to call my sister or my mom back, and I’ll think, “I’ll do it after I finish this one thing,” and then it’s just gone. Or I’ll see a bill and think, “Okay, I need to pay that tonight,” and sometimes I even set a reminder, and then the reminder pops up and I either dismiss it because I’m in the middle of something or I think I’ll come back to it, and then I don’t. And then suddenly it’s late, and I’m like… how did I let that happen when I literally knew about it?

Same kind of thing with appointments. I don’t usually mean to miss things, obviously, but I’ll get the text reminder or put it in my calendar, and somehow I’ll still misjudge it or not transition to actually going. It feels like the step between remembering and doing is where everything falls apart.

And even outside of home stuff, like errands or follow-ups — if there’s a missing order or something I’m supposed to check on, I’ll have the intention, and then unless it’s right in front of my face at the exact moment, it can just disappear from my brain. It’s really frustrating because it’s not that I don’t care. I do care. I just don’t seem to reliably hold onto the task long enough to complete it.

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Aside from these attention and focus issues, have you been experiencing persistent low mood, loss of interest in things you used to enjoy, or excessive worry over the past couple of weeks?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? At least not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think. I mean, I get frustrated and overwhelmed because I feel like I’m constantly messing something up or having to work twice as hard to keep up, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been persistently sad for weeks or that I’ve stopped caring about things I normally like.\n\nAnd worry… I mean, I do worry about work and whether I’m missing something, but I’m not sure if that’s “excessive worry” or just reacting to what’s happening, if that makes sense. It doesn’t feel like the main thing is mood or anxiety. It feels more like I’m exhausted from trying to keep my head organized all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically restless, fidgety, or have trouble sitting still, or find yourself acting or speaking impulsively without thinking it through?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know that I’d describe myself that way, no. Like, I’m not usually the person who can’t stay in a chair or is pacing around because I physically can’t stop moving. I mean, I’ll shift around or play with a pen sometimes, but I always thought that was just normal, especially during long meetings or charting or whatever.\n\nAnd impulsive speaking… I’m trying to think. I don’t think people usually experience me as blurting things out or interrupting a ton. If anything, I’m more likely to be kind of stuck in my head trying to organize what I’m going to say before I say it. Sometimes I’ll talk too much once I get going — like I’m probably doing right now — but it doesn’t feel like I’m saying reckless things without thinking, exactly.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think the restless or impulsive part is the main thing for me. Or at least it’s not something I’ve noticed the way I notice the focus stuff.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you notice these same kinds of attention or focus problems causing issues in other areas of your life right now, like at home, in relationships, or when driving?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It’s not just work. Work is just where the consequences feel bigger, so it’s more obvious and more embarrassing.\n\nAt home it’s things that should be so routine that I feel ridiculous even saying them. Like I’ll fully intend to call my sister or my mom back, and I’ll think, “I’ll do it after I finish this one thing,” and then it’s just gone. Or I’ll see a bill and think, “Okay, I need to pay that tonight,” and sometimes I even set a reminder, and then the reminder pops up and I either dismiss it because I’m in the middle of something or I think I’ll come back to it, and then I don’t. And then suddenly it’s late, and I’m like… how did I let that happen when I literally knew about it?\n\nSame kind of thing with appointments. I don’t usually mean to miss things, obviously, but I’ll get the text reminder or put it in my calendar, and somehow I’ll still misjudge it or not transition to actually going. It feels like the step between remembering and doing is where everything falls apart.\n\nAnd even outside of home stuff, like errands or follow-ups — if there’s a missing order or something I’m supposed to check on, I’ll have the intention, and then unless it’s right in front of my face at the exact moment, it can just disappear from my brain. It’s really frustrating because it’s not that I don’t care. I do care. I just don’t seem to reliably hold onto the task long enough to complete it.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow has your sleep been lately—are you getting enough restful sleep most nights, or do you struggle with insomnia or feel excessively tired during the day?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / registered_nurse / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um… I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t think I’ve been tracking it closely enough to say in a clean way, like “yes, restful” or “no, insomnia.” Some nights I feel like I sleep okay, and other nights I don’t feel especially refreshed, but I don’t know if that’s because of sleep itself or just everything feeling like it takes more effort during the day.

I wouldn’t say I’m lying awake for hours every night in a way that feels like classic insomnia, at least not that I’ve noticed as the main issue. And I don’t think I’m falling asleep uncontrollably during the day or anything like that. I do feel tired sometimes, but I guess I haven’t really separated out whether that’s lack of sleep or just mental fatigue.

So… I don’t have a great answer. I haven’t noticed a major sleep problem, but I also probably haven’t been paying close enough attention to be completely confident.

I don’t think I’ve been tracking it closely enough to say in a clean way, like “yes, restful” or “no, insomnia.” Some nights I feel like I sleep okay, and other nights I don’t feel especially refreshed, but I don’t know if that’s because of sleep itself or just everything feeling like it takes more effort during the day.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m lying awake for hours every night in a way that feels like classic insomnia, at least not that I’ve noticed as the main issue. And I don’t think I’m falling asleep uncontrollably during the day or anything like that. I do feel tired sometimes, but I guess I haven’t really separated out whether that’s lack of sleep or just mental fatigue.\n\nSo… I don’t have a great answer. I haven’t noticed a major sleep problem, but I also probably haven’t been paying close enough attention to be completely confident.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself avoiding, procrastinating on, or feeling unusually reluctant to start tasks that require sustained mental effort—like detailed paperwork, charting, or planning—because they feel especially draining or hard to stick with?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Like I’ll fully intend to call my sister or my mom back, and I’ll think, “I’ll do it after I finish this one thing,” and then it’s just gone. Or I’ll see a bill and think, “Okay, I need to pay that tonight,” and sometimes I even set a reminder, and then the reminder pops up and I either dismiss it because I’m in the middle of something or I think I’ll come back to it, and then I don’t.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. That’s actually one of the biggest things I notice.

If I know something is going to require sustained focus — especially something like detailed chart review, auditing documentation across shifts, or sitting down to compare what happened on one shift to what was documented on another — I will put it off in this way that feels almost physical. Like I know it needs to be done, and I’m not confused about how to do it, but sitting down to start it feels like trying to climb a wall.

And then I’ll suddenly find all these other things that feel more urgent or more doable, especially bedside tasks. I’ll be like, “Well, I should check on this patient,” or “Let me restock this,” or “I’ll answer this call light first,” and those are real tasks, so it’s not like I’m doing nothing. But if I’m being honest, I’m also using them to avoid the thing that needs that kind of long, focused mental effort.

It’s not even that the paperwork or review is always hard intellectually. It’s more that staying with it feels so draining, and I can’t seem to get myself to settle into it unless there’s a lot of pressure or someone is basically waiting on it. Then I feel guilty, because I know I’ve been pushing it off, but in the moment it just feels overwhelmingly hard to start.

for older adolescents and adults, returning calls, paying bills, keeping appointments).\n  manifestation: I forget things that should be routine, like calling a family member back, following up on a missing order, paying a bill at home, or showing up for an appointment, even when I meant to do it and sometimes even after I set a reminder.\n- key: S015\n  name: Excessive_Talking\n  description: Often talks excessively.\n  manifestation: During handoff or when I’m mentoring a newer nurse, I catch myself giving way too much background and talking in circles until people are glancing at the clock or trying to jump in, and I still have trouble wrapping it up.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Even in elementary school, my teachers wrote that I was bright but careless and always losing worksheets or forgetting to turn in homework, and my mom had to sit with me every night to keep me on track.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: At work, the concerns have gotten serious enough that I’m on formal supervision and other nurses have to double-check parts of my charge-nurse follow-through, which makes me feel like I’m becoming a liability instead of a leader.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 11 months\n  manifestation: This has been a major problem since I took on the expanded charge-nurse responsibilities almost two years ago.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / registered_nurse / graduate\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S008\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you notice these same kinds of attention or focus problems causing issues in other areas of your life right now, like at home, in relationships, or when driving?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It’s not just work. Work is just where the consequences feel bigger, so it’s more obvious and more embarrassing.\n\nAt home it’s things that should be so routine that I feel ridiculous even saying them. Like I’ll fully intend to call my sister or my mom back, and I’ll think, “I’ll do it after I finish this one thing,” and then it’s just gone. Or I’ll see a bill and think, “Okay, I need to pay that tonight,” and sometimes I even set a reminder, and then the reminder pops up and I either dismiss it because I’m in the middle of something or I think I’ll come back to it, and then I don’t. And then suddenly it’s late, and I’m like… how did I let that happen when I literally knew about it?\n\nSame kind of thing with appointments. I don’t usually mean to miss things, obviously, but I’ll get the text reminder or put it in my calendar, and somehow I’ll still misjudge it or not transition to actually going. It feels like the step between remembering and doing is where everything falls apart.\n\nAnd even outside of home stuff, like errands or follow-ups — if there’s a missing order or something I’m supposed to check on, I’ll have the intention, and then unless it’s right in front of my face at the exact moment, it can just disappear from my brain. It’s really frustrating because it’s not that I don’t care. I do care. I just don’t seem to reliably hold onto the task long enough to complete it.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been lately—are you getting enough restful sleep most nights, or do you struggle with insomnia or feel excessively tired during the day?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t think I’ve been tracking it closely enough to say in a clean way, like “yes, restful” or “no, insomnia.” Some nights I feel like I sleep okay, and other nights I don’t feel especially refreshed, but I don’t know if that’s because of sleep itself or just everything feeling like it takes more effort during the day.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m lying awake for hours every night in a way that feels like classic insomnia, at least not that I’ve noticed as the main issue. And I don’t think I’m falling asleep uncontrollably during the day or anything like that. I do feel tired sometimes, but I guess I haven’t really separated out whether that’s lack of sleep or just mental fatigue.\n\nSo… I don’t have a great answer. I haven’t noticed a major sleep problem, but I also probably haven’t been paying close enough attention to be completely confident.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding, procrastinating on, or feeling unusually reluctant to start tasks that require sustained mental effort—like detailed paperwork, charting, or planning—because they feel especially draining or hard to stick with?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the biggest things I notice.\n\nIf I know something is going to require sustained focus — especially something like detailed chart review, auditing documentation across shifts, or sitting down to compare what happened on one shift to what was documented on another — I will put it off in this way that feels almost physical. Like I know it needs to be done, and I’m not confused about how to do it, but sitting down to start it feels like trying to climb a wall.\n\nAnd then I’ll suddenly find all these other things that feel more urgent or more doable, especially bedside tasks. I’ll be like, “Well, I should check on this patient,” or “Let me restock this,” or “I’ll answer this call light first,” and those are real tasks, so it’s not like I’m doing nothing. But if I’m being honest, I’m also using them to avoid the thing that needs that kind of long, focused mental effort.\n\nIt’s not even that the paperwork or review is always hard intellectually. It’s more that staying with it feels so draining, and I can’t seem to get myself to settle into it unless there’s a lot of pressure or someone is basically waiting on it. Then I feel guilty, because I know I’ve been pushing it off, but in the moment it just feels overwhelmingly hard to start.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you regularly use caffeine, nicotine, alcohol, or any recreational drugs, and if so, has your attention or focus changed noticeably when you use more or less of them?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. I’m not totally sure how to answer that in a useful way. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, welcome in. I'm Dr. Lee. To start, can you tell me what's been bringing you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, Dr. Lee. Um… I guess the biggest reason I’m here is that at work, on the floor, I’m losing my train of thought constantly, and it’s starting to feel like I can’t trust my own brain in the middle of the day.\n\nLike, I’ll be trying to work through a patient flow issue — figuring out who needs to go where, or what needs to happen next — and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone near me asks another nurse a question, or a call light goes off, and it’s like whatever I was holding in my head just disappears. Not just “oh, give me a second,” but genuinely gone, and I have to retrace everything to figure out what I was doing. It happens over and over, and because the floor is basically constant interruptions, I feel like I’m always getting knocked off track.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed this kind of difficulty holding onto your train of thought throughout your life, even back in school or earlier jobs, or is this something that's really only started recently?\n\nPatient: No, it’s definitely not just recent. I mean, work is where it feels most scary now because there are actual patients involved, but when I think back, it’s been there for as long as I can remember.\n\nEven in elementary school, teachers would write things like, “She’s bright, but careless,” or that I wasn’t turning things in. And I remember constantly losing worksheets — like I’d do them, sometimes, or at least start them, and then they’d just vanish into my desk or backpack. Homework was a whole thing too. My mom basically had to sit with me every night and keep redirecting me, like, “Okay, what’s next? Did you put it in your folder? Did you put the folder in your backpack?” Otherwise I’d either drift off or forget to turn it in even if I’d done it.\n\nSo… yeah, the setting is different now, but that pattern of losing the thread or needing someone to keep me on track has kind of always been there.\n\nDoctor: Aside from these attention and focus issues, have you been experiencing persistent low mood, loss of interest in things you used to enjoy, or excessive worry over the past couple of weeks?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? At least not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think. I mean, I get frustrated and overwhelmed because I feel like I’m constantly messing something up or having to work twice as hard to keep up, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been persistently sad for weeks or that I’ve stopped caring about things I normally like.\n\nAnd worry… I mean, I do worry about work and whether I’m missing something, but I’m not sure if that’s “excessive worry” or just reacting to what’s happening, if that makes sense. It doesn’t feel like the main thing is mood or anxiety. It feels more like I’m exhausted from trying to keep my head organized all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically restless, fidgety, or have trouble sitting still, or find yourself acting or speaking impulsively without thinking it through?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know that I’d describe myself that way, no. Like, I’m not usually the person who can’t stay in a chair or is pacing around because I physically can’t stop moving. I mean, I’ll shift around or play with a pen sometimes, but I always thought that was just normal, especially during long meetings or charting or whatever.\n\nAnd impulsive speaking… I’m trying to think. I don’t think people usually experience me as blurting things out or interrupting a ton. If anything, I’m more likely to be kind of stuck in my head trying to organize what I’m going to say before I say it. Sometimes I’ll talk too much once I get going — like I’m probably doing right now — but it doesn’t feel like I’m saying reckless things without thinking, exactly.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think the restless or impulsive part is the main thing for me. Or at least it’s not something I’ve noticed the way I notice the focus stuff.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you notice these same kinds of attention or focus problems causing issues in other areas of your life right now, like at home, in relationships, or when driving?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It’s not just work. Work is just where the consequences feel bigger, so it’s more obvious and more embarrassing.\n\nAt home it’s things that should be so routine that I feel ridiculous even saying them. Like I’ll fully intend to call my sister or my mom back, and I’ll think, “I’ll do it after I finish this one thing,” and then it’s just gone. Or I’ll see a bill and think, “Okay, I need to pay that tonight,” and sometimes I even set a reminder, and then the reminder pops up and I either dismiss it because I’m in the middle of something or I think I’ll come back to it, and then I don’t. And then suddenly it’s late, and I’m like… how did I let that happen when I literally knew about it?\n\nSame kind of thing with appointments. It feels like the step between remembering and doing is where everything falls apart.\n\nAnd even outside of home stuff, like errands or follow-ups — if there’s a missing order or something I’m supposed to check on, I’ll have the intention, and then unless it’s right in front of my face at the exact moment, it can just disappear from my brain. It’s really frustrating because it’s not that I don’t care. I do care. I just don’t seem to reliably hold onto the task long enough to complete it.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been lately—are you getting enough restful sleep most nights, or do you struggle with insomnia or feel excessively tired during the day?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t think I’ve been tracking it closely enough to say in a clean way, like “yes, restful” or “no, insomnia.” Some nights I feel like I sleep okay, and other nights I don’t feel especially refreshed, but I don’t know if that’s because of sleep itself or just everything feeling like it takes more effort during the day.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m lying awake for hours every night in a way that feels like classic insomnia, at least not that I’ve noticed as the main issue. And I don’t think I’m falling asleep uncontrollably during the day or anything like that. I do feel tired sometimes, but I guess I haven’t really separated out whether that’s lack of sleep or just mental fatigue.\n\nSo… I don’t have a great answer. It feels more like I’m exhausted from trying to keep my head organized all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically restless, fidgety, or have trouble sitting still, or find yourself acting or speaking impulsively without thinking it through?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know that I’d describe myself that way, no. Like, I’m not usually the person who can’t stay in a chair or is pacing around because I physically can’t stop moving. I mean, I’ll shift around or play with a pen sometimes, but I always thought that was just normal, especially during long meetings or charting or whatever.\n\nAnd impulsive speaking… I’m trying to think. I don’t think people usually experience me as blurting things out or interrupting a ton. If anything, I’m more likely to be kind of stuck in my head trying to organize what I’m going to say before I say it. Sometimes I’ll talk too much once I get going — like I’m probably doing right now — but it doesn’t feel like I’m saying reckless things without thinking, exactly.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think the restless or impulsive part is the main thing for me. Or at least it’s not something I’ve noticed the way I notice the focus stuff.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you notice these same kinds of attention or focus problems causing issues in other areas of your life right now, like at home, in relationships, or when driving?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It’s not just work. Work is just where the consequences feel bigger, so it’s more obvious and more embarrassing.\n\nAt home it’s things that should be so routine that I feel ridiculous even saying them. Like I’ll fully intend to call my sister or my mom back, and I’ll think, “I’ll do it after I finish this one thing,” and then it’s just gone. Or I’ll see a bill and think, “Okay, I need to pay that tonight,” and sometimes I even set a reminder, and then the reminder pops up and I either dismiss it because I’m in the middle of something or I think I’ll come back to it, and then I don’t. And then suddenly it’s late, and I’m like… how did I let that happen when I literally knew about it?\n\nSame kind of thing with appointments. I don’t usually mean to miss things, obviously, but I’ll get the text reminder or put it in my calendar, and somehow I’ll still misjudge it or not transition to actually going. It feels like the step between remembering and doing is where everything falls apart.\n\nAnd even outside of home stuff, like errands or follow-ups — if there’s a missing order or something I’m supposed to check on, I’ll have the intention, and then unless it’s right in front of my face at the exact moment, it can just disappear from my brain. It’s really frustrating because it’s not that I don’t care. I do care. I just don’t seem to reliably hold onto the task long enough to complete it.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been lately—are you getting enough restful sleep most nights, or do you struggle with insomnia or feel excessively tired during the day?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t think I’ve been tracking it closely enough to say in a clean way, like “yes, restful” or “no, insomnia.” Some nights I feel like I sleep okay, and other nights I don’t feel especially refreshed, but I don’t know if that’s because of sleep itself or just everything feeling like it takes more effort during the day.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m lying awake for hours every night in a way that feels like classic insomnia, at least not that I’ve noticed as the main issue. And I don’t think I’m falling asleep uncontrollably during the day or anything like that. I do feel tired sometimes, but I guess I haven’t really separated out whether that’s lack of sleep or just mental fatigue.\n\nSo… I don’t have a great answer. I haven’t noticed a major sleep problem, but I also probably haven’t been paying close enough attention to be completely confident.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding, procrastinating on, or feeling unusually reluctant to start tasks that require sustained mental effort—like detailed paperwork, charting, or planning—because they feel especially draining or hard to stick with?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the biggest things I notice.\n\nIf I know something is going to require sustained focus — especially something like detailed chart review, auditing documentation across shifts, or sitting down to compare what happened on one shift to what was documented on another — I will put it off in this way that feels almost physical. Like I know it needs to be done, and I’m not confused about how to do it, but sitting down to start it feels like trying to climb a wall.\n\nAnd then I’ll suddenly find all these other things that feel more urgent or more doable, especially bedside tasks. I’ll be like, “Well, I should check on this patient,” or “Let me restock this,” or “I’ll answer this call light first,” and those are real tasks, so it’s not like I’m doing nothing. But if I’m being honest, I’m also using them to avoid the thing that needs that kind of long, focused mental effort.\n\nIt’s not even that the paperwork or review is always hard intellectually. It’s more that staying with it feels so draining, and I can’t seem to get myself to settle into it unless there’s a lot of pressure or someone is basically waiting on it. Then I feel guilty, because I know I’ve been pushing it off, but in the moment it just feels overwhelmingly hard to start.\n\nDoctor: Do you regularly use caffeine, nicotine, alcohol, or any recreational drugs, and if so, has your attention or focus changed noticeably when you use more or less of them?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that in a useful way. I don’t think I’ve paid close enough attention to notice a clear pattern, like, “when I have more caffeine my focus is better,” or “when I have less it’s worse.” I mean, I probably use caffeine the way a lot of nurses do, but I haven’t tracked it in any deliberate way, and I’d be guessing if I tried to connect it directly to my attention.\n\nAs far as the other things you mentioned, I don’t have anything specific I can point to where I’ve noticed my focus changing because of using more or less of something. It’s more that my attention problems feel pretty consistently there, and I haven’t really identified a substance-related pattern. If you want, I can try to think through it more specifically, but nothing obvious comes to mind.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think I can focus better when something is interesting, but it’s not like my attention is totally reliable even then. The bigger issue, and honestly the main reason I’m here, is what happens on the floor.

I’ll be in the middle of thinking through a patient flow issue — like who needs to be moved, what needs to happen next, what I was about to communicate — and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone asks another nurse a question near me, or a call light goes off, and it’s like my whole thought just disappears. Not “I’m a little distracted,” but literally I’ll stop and think, wait, what was I just doing?

And then I have to mentally retrace everything: where I was standing, which patient I was thinking about, what I had already done, what I was about to do next. It’s embarrassing because the interruptions are normal hospital noises — they’re not unusual — but they derail me so fast. Even if I was focused a second before, one external thing can pull me off track, and then I’m scrambling to get back to the original thread.

Um… I guess the biggest reason I’m here is that at work, on the floor, I’m losing my train of thought constantly, and it’s starting to feel like I can’t trust my own brain in the middle of the day.\n\nLike, I’ll be trying to work through a patient flow issue — figuring out who needs to go where, or what needs to happen next — and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone near me asks another nurse a question, or a call light goes off, and it’s like whatever I was holding in my head just disappears. I mean, work is where it feels most scary now because there are actual patients involved, but when I think back, it’s been there for as long as I can remember.\n\nEven in elementary school, teachers would write things like, “She’s bright, but careless,” or that I wasn’t turning things in. My mom basically had to sit with me every night and keep redirecting me, like, “Okay, what’s next? Did you put it in your folder? At least not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think. It doesn’t feel like the main thing is mood or anxiety. It feels more like I’m exhausted from trying to keep my head organized all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically restless, fidgety, or have trouble sitting still, or find yourself acting or speaking impulsively without thinking it through?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know that I’d describe myself that way, no. Like, I’m not usually the person who can’t stay in a chair or is pacing around because I physically can’t stop moving. I mean, I’ll shift around or play with a pen sometimes, but I always thought that was just normal, especially during long meetings or charting or whatever.\n\nAnd impulsive speaking… I’m trying to think. I don’t think people usually experience me as blurting things out or interrupting a ton. If anything, I’m more likely to be kind of stuck in my head trying to organize what I’m going to say before I say it. Sometimes I’ll talk too much once I get going — like I’m probably doing right now — but it doesn’t feel like I’m saying reckless things without thinking, exactly.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think the restless or impulsive part is the main thing for me. Or at least it’s not something I’ve noticed the way I notice the focus stuff.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you notice these same kinds of attention or focus problems causing issues in other areas of your life right now, like at home, in relationships, or when driving?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It’s not just work. Work is just where the consequences feel bigger, so it’s more obvious and more embarrassing.\n\nAt home it’s things that should be so routine that I feel ridiculous even saying them. Like I’ll fully intend to call my sister or my mom back, and I’ll think, “I’ll do it after I finish this one thing,” and then it’s just gone. Or I’ll see a bill and think, “Okay, I need to pay that tonight,” and sometimes I even set a reminder, and then the reminder pops up and I either dismiss it because I’m in the middle of something or I think I’ll come back to it, and then I don’t. And then suddenly it’s late, and I’m like… how did I let that happen when I literally knew about it?\n\nSame kind of thing with appointments. I don’t usually mean to miss things, obviously, but I’ll get the text reminder or put it in my calendar, and somehow I’ll still misjudge it or not transition to actually going. It feels like the step between remembering and doing is where everything falls apart.\n\nAnd even outside of home stuff, like errands or follow-ups — if there’s a missing order or something I’m supposed to check on, I’ll have the intention, and then unless it’s right in front of my face at the exact moment, it can just disappear from my brain. It’s really frustrating because it’s not that I don’t care. I do care. I just don’t seem to reliably hold onto the task long enough to complete it.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been lately—are you getting enough restful sleep most nights, or do you struggle with insomnia or feel excessively tired during the day?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t think I’ve been tracking it closely enough to say in a clean way, like “yes, restful” or “no, insomnia.” Some nights I feel like I sleep okay, and other nights I don’t feel especially refreshed, but I don’t know if that’s because of sleep itself or just everything feeling like it takes more effort during the day.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m lying awake for hours every night in a way that feels like classic insomnia, at least not that I’ve noticed as the main issue. And I don’t think I’m falling asleep uncontrollably during the day or anything like that. I do feel tired sometimes, but I guess I haven’t really separated out whether that’s lack of sleep or just mental fatigue.\n\nSo… I don’t have a great answer. I haven’t noticed a major sleep problem, but I also probably haven’t been paying close enough attention to be completely confident.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding, procrastinating on, or feeling unusually reluctant to start tasks that require sustained mental effort—like detailed paperwork, charting, or planning—because they feel especially draining or hard to stick with?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the biggest things I notice.\n\nIf I know something is going to require sustained focus — especially something like detailed chart review, auditing documentation across shifts, or sitting down to compare what happened on one shift to what was documented on another — I will put it off in this way that feels almost physical. Like I know it needs to be done, and I’m not confused about how to do it, but sitting down to start it feels like trying to climb a wall.\n\nAnd then I’ll suddenly find all these other things that feel more urgent or more doable, especially bedside tasks. I’ll be like, “Well, I should check on this patient,” or “Let me restock this,” or “I’ll answer this call light first,” and those are real tasks, so it’s not like I’m doing nothing. But if I’m being honest, I’m also using them to avoid the thing that needs that kind of long, focused mental effort.\n\nIt’s not even that the paperwork or review is always hard intellectually. It’s more that staying with it feels so draining, and I can’t seem to get myself to settle into it unless there’s a lot of pressure or someone is basically waiting on it. Then I feel guilty, because I know I’ve been pushing it off, but in the moment it just feels overwhelmingly hard to start.\n\nDoctor: Do you regularly use caffeine, nicotine, alcohol, or any recreational drugs, and if so, has your attention or focus changed noticeably when you use more or less of them?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that in a useful way. I don’t think I’ve paid close enough attention to notice a clear pattern, like, “when I have more caffeine my focus is better,” or “when I have less it’s worse.” I mean, I probably use caffeine the way a lot of nurses do, but I haven’t tracked it in any deliberate way, and I’d be guessing if I tried to connect it directly to my attention.\n\nAs far as the other things you mentioned, I don’t have anything specific I can point to where I’ve noticed my focus changing because of using more or less of something. It’s more that my attention problems feel pretty consistently there, and I haven’t really identified a substance-related pattern. If you want, I can try to think through it more specifically, but nothing obvious comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something you actually enjoy or find interesting—like a hobby, a good book, or a show—do you still have trouble maintaining focus, or can you concentrate just fine in those situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I can focus better when something is interesting, but it’s not like my attention is totally reliable even then. The bigger issue, and honestly the main reason I’m here, is what happens on the floor.\n\nI’ll be in the middle of thinking through a patient flow issue — like who needs to be moved, what needs to happen next, what I was about to communicate — and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone asks another nurse a question near me, or a call light goes off, and it’s like my whole thought just disappears. Not “I’m a little distracted,” but literally I’ll stop and think, wait, what was I just doing?\n\nAnd then I have to mentally retrace everything: where I was standing, which patient I was thinking about, what I had already done, what I was about to do next. It’s embarrassing because the interruptions are normal hospital noises — they’re not unusual — but they derail me so fast. Even if I was focused a second before, one external thing can pull me off track, and then I’m scrambling to get back to the original thread.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

No new differential diagnoses introduced.", "is_final": false}

Not “I’m a little distracted,” but literally I’ll stop and think, wait, what was I just doing?\n\nAnd then I have to mentally retrace everything: where I was standing, which patient I was thinking about, what I had already done, what I was about to do next. Even if I was focused a second before, one external thing can pull me off track, and then I’m scrambling to get back to the original thread.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave these attention lapses ever led to an actual mistake, near-miss, or safety concern with a patient, or gotten you into trouble at work?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / registered_nurse / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"At work, the concerns have gotten serious enough that I’m on formal supervision and other nurses have to double-check parts of my charge-nurse follow-through, which makes me feel like I’m becoming a liability instead of a leader.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I mean… I hate saying that, but yes, it’s gotten to the point where work is formally involved.

I’m on supervision right now, and part of that is because people are worried about my follow-through when I’m in a charge role. It’s not like I don’t care, or I’m blowing things off on purpose — I care a lot, probably too much — but there have been enough issues with me missing steps or not closing the loop on things that other nurses now have to double-check parts of what I’m supposed to be managing.

And that feels awful. Because as charge nurse, I’m supposed to be the person keeping the big picture together and making sure nothing falls through the cracks. Instead, I feel like people are watching behind me to make sure I didn’t miss something. I understand why they’re doing it, honestly, but it’s humiliating and scary.

I don’t want to make it sound like I’m saying there was one huge catastrophic event, but the safety concern is real enough that my workplace is treating it seriously. And emotionally, it’s been really hard because I used to think of myself as reliable and capable, and now I feel like I’m becoming a liability instead of a leader.

I'm Dr. Lee. To start, can you tell me what's been bringing you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, Dr. Lee. Um… I guess the biggest reason I’m here is that at work, on the floor, I’m losing my train of thought constantly, and it’s starting to feel like I can’t trust my own brain in the middle of the day.\n\nLike, I’ll be trying to work through a patient flow issue — figuring out who needs to go where, or what needs to happen next — and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone near me asks another nurse a question, or a call light goes off, and it’s like whatever I was holding in my head just disappears. Not just “oh, give me a second,” but genuinely gone, and I have to retrace everything to figure out what I was doing. It happens over and over, and because the floor is basically constant interruptions, I feel like I’m always getting knocked off track.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed this kind of difficulty holding onto your train of thought throughout your life, even back in school or earlier jobs, or is this something that's really only started recently?\n\nPatient: No, it’s definitely not just recent. I mean, work is where it feels most scary now because there are actual patients involved, but when I think back, it’s been there for as long as I can remember.\n\nEven in elementary school, teachers would write things like, “She’s bright, but careless,” or that I wasn’t turning things in. And I remember constantly losing worksheets — like I’d do them, sometimes, or at least start them, and then they’d just vanish into my desk or backpack. Homework was a whole thing too. My mom basically had to sit with me every night and keep redirecting me, like, “Okay, what’s next? Did you put it in your folder? Did you put the folder in your backpack?” Otherwise I’d either drift off or forget to turn it in even if I’d done it.\n\nSo… yeah, the setting is different now, but that pattern of losing the thread or needing someone to keep me on track has kind of always been there.\n\nDoctor: Aside from these attention and focus issues, have you been experiencing persistent low mood, loss of interest in things you used to enjoy, or excessive worry over the past couple of weeks?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? At least not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think. It doesn’t feel like the main thing is mood or anxiety. It feels more like I’m exhausted from trying to keep my head organized all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically restless, fidgety, or have trouble sitting still, or find yourself acting or speaking impulsively without thinking it through?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know that I’d describe myself that way, no. Like, I’m not usually the person who can’t stay in a chair or is pacing around because I physically can’t stop moving. I mean, I’ll shift around or play with a pen sometimes, but I always thought that was just normal, especially during long meetings or charting or whatever.\n\nAnd impulsive speaking… I’m trying to think. I don’t think people usually experience me as blurting things out or interrupting a ton. If anything, I’m more likely to be kind of stuck in my head trying to organize what I’m going to say before I say it. Sometimes I’ll talk too much once I get going — like I’m probably doing right now — but it doesn’t feel like I’m saying reckless things without thinking, exactly.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think the restless or impulsive part is the main thing for me. Or at least it’s not something I’ve noticed the way I notice the focus stuff.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you notice these same kinds of attention or focus problems causing issues in other areas of your life right now, like at home, in relationships, or when driving?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It’s not just work. Work is just where the consequences feel bigger, so it’s more obvious and more embarrassing.\n\nAt home it’s things that should be so routine that I feel ridiculous even saying them. Like I’ll fully intend to call my sister or my mom back, and I’ll think, “I’ll do it after I finish this one thing,” and then it’s just gone. Or I’ll see a bill and think, “Okay, I need to pay that tonight,” and sometimes I even set a reminder, and then the reminder pops up and I either dismiss it because I’m in the middle of something or I think I’ll come back to it, and then I don’t. And then suddenly it’s late, and I’m like… how did I let that happen when I literally knew about it?\n\nSame kind of thing with appointments. I don’t usually mean to miss things, obviously, but I’ll get the text reminder or put it in my calendar, and somehow I’ll still misjudge it or not transition to actually going. It feels like the step between remembering and doing is where everything falls apart.\n\nAnd even outside of home stuff, like errands or follow-ups — if there’s a missing order or something I’m supposed to check on, I’ll have the intention, and then unless it’s right in front of my face at the exact moment, it can just disappear from my brain. It’s really frustrating because it’s not that I don’t care. I do care. I just don’t seem to reliably hold onto the task long enough to complete it.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been lately—are you getting enough restful sleep most nights, or do you struggle with insomnia or feel excessively tired during the day?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t think I’ve been tracking it closely enough to say in a clean way, like “yes, restful” or “no, insomnia.” Some nights I feel like I sleep okay, and other nights I don’t feel especially refreshed, but I don’t know if that’s because of sleep itself or just everything feeling like it takes more effort during the day.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m lying awake for hours every night in a way that feels like classic insomnia, at least not that I’ve noticed as the main issue. And I don’t think I’m falling asleep uncontrollably during the day or anything like that. I do feel tired sometimes, but I guess I haven’t really separated out whether that’s lack of sleep or just mental fatigue.\n\nSo… I don’t have a great answer. I haven’t noticed a major sleep problem, but I also probably haven’t been paying close enough attention to be completely confident.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding, procrastinating on, or feeling unusually reluctant to start tasks that require sustained mental effort—like detailed paperwork, charting, or planning—because they feel especially draining or hard to stick with?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the biggest things I notice.\n\nIf I know something is going to require sustained focus — especially something like detailed chart review, auditing documentation across shifts, or sitting down to compare what happened on one shift to what was documented on another — I will put it off in this way that feels almost physical. Like I know it needs to be done, and I’m not confused about how to do it, but sitting down to start it feels like trying to climb a wall.\n\nAnd then I’ll suddenly find all these other things that feel more urgent or more doable, especially bedside tasks. I’ll be like, “Well, I should check on this patient,” or “Let me restock this,” or “I’ll answer this call light first,” and those are real tasks, so it’s not like I’m doing nothing. But if I’m being honest, I’m also using them to avoid the thing that needs that kind of long, focused mental effort.\n\nIt’s not even that the paperwork or review is always hard intellectually. It’s more that staying with it feels so draining, and I can’t seem to get myself to settle into it unless there’s a lot of pressure or someone is basically waiting on it. Then I feel guilty, because I know I’ve been pushing it off, but in the moment it just feels overwhelmingly hard to start.\n\nDoctor: Do you regularly use caffeine, nicotine, alcohol, or any recreational drugs, and if so, has your attention or focus changed noticeably when you use more or less of them?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that in a useful way. I don’t think I’ve paid close enough attention to notice a clear pattern, like, “when I have more caffeine my focus is better,” or “when I have less it’s worse.” I mean, I probably use caffeine the way a lot of nurses do, but I haven’t tracked it in any deliberate way, and I’d be guessing if I tried to connect it directly to my attention.\n\nAs far as the other things you mentioned, I don’t have anything specific I can point to where I’ve noticed my focus changing because of using more or less of something. ---------- turn 11 | patient:alignment | patient.build_alignment_messages() | model=gpt-5.5 ----------

  {

Because as charge nurse, I’m supposed to be the person keeping the big picture together and making sure nothing falls through the cracks. Instead, I feel like people are watching behind me to make sure I didn’t miss something. I understand why they’re doing it, honestly, but it’s humiliating and scary.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I’m saying there was one huge catastrophic event, but the safety concern is real enough that my workplace is treating it seriously. Yeah… unfortunately, yes. At work it’s gotten serious enough that I’m not just privately worrying about it anymore — it’s become an actual formal thing. I’m on supervision right now, and part of what they’re watching is my follow-through when I’m in the charge nurse role.

Um… I guess the biggest reason I’m here is that at work, on the floor, I’m losing my train of thought constantly, and it’s starting to feel like I can’t trust my own brain in the middle of the day.\n\nLike, I’ll be trying to work through a patient flow issue — figuring out who needs to go where, or what needs to happen next — and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone near me asks another nurse a question, or a call light goes off, and it’s like whatever I was holding in my head just disappears. Not just “oh, give me a second,” but genuinely gone, and I have to retrace everything to figure out what I was doing. It happens over and over, and because the floor is basically constant interruptions, I feel like I’m always getting knocked off track.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed this kind of difficulty holding onto your train of thought throughout your life, even back in school or earlier jobs, or is this something that's really only started recently?\n\nPatient: No, it’s definitely not just recent. I mean, work is where it feels most scary now because there are actual patients involved, but when I think back, it’s been there for as long as I can remember.\n\nEven in elementary school, teachers would write things like, “She’s bright, but careless,” or that I wasn’t turning things in. My mom basically had to sit with me every night and keep redirecting me, like, “Okay, what’s next? Did you put it in your folder? Did you put the folder in your backpack?” Otherwise I’d either drift off or forget to turn it in even if I’d done it.\n\nSo… yeah, the setting is different now, but that pattern of losing the thread or needing someone to keep me on track has kind of always been there.\n\nDoctor: Aside from these attention and focus issues, have you been experiencing persistent low mood, loss of interest in things you used to enjoy, or excessive worry over the past couple of weeks?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick). Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food). Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. It’s really frustrating because it’s not that I don’t care. I do care.
